İngilizce aslı

What was the first Qurʾānic revelation?

Turns on: The competing reports, and whether 'first' means first verses, complete sūra, public warning, or after a pause.

  1. The opening of al-ʿAlaq began revelation. — Aisha's true-dream, Ḥirāʾ, and three-embrace report is transmitted in both Ṣaḥīḥs according to al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr; al-Wāḥidī and Ibn ʿAṭiyya prefer it. Al-Burhān cites authenticated Aisha and Abū Rajāʾ reports; Muslim's version extends to 96:5, Bukhārī's abbreviated version to 96:3. BURHAN (بعضهم): Another distinction says اقرأ باسم ربك was first for prophethood, while يا أيها المدثر was first for messengership, since the latter commands a general public warning.; cites 74:1 BURHAN (مجاهد): Mujāhid says the first sūra revealed was اقرأ, followed by Nūḥ.; cites 71:1 BIQAI: While the Prophet was devoted to solitude at Ḥirāʾ, Gabriel came with the first five verses of this sūra, ending with the phrase about what he did not know. BIQAI-FULL: The active verb is placed before its prepositional phrase because this is more forceful, especially in the first revelation, where the command to read is paramount. DURR (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that “Read in the name of your Lord” was the first Qurʾān revealed in Mecca. DURR (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās separately reports that this was the first sūra revealed to Muhammad. DURR (مجاهد): Mujāhid says Sūrat al-ʿAlaq was the first revelation, followed by “Nūn wa-l-qalam.” DURR (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says the first revelation consisted of five verses, ending with “what he did not know.” DURR (عبيد بن عمير): ʿUbayd b. ʿUmayr says al-ʿAlaq was first, followed by Sūrat Nūn. DURR (عائشة): ʿĀʾisha says the sūras revealed after al-ʿAlaq included Nūn wa-l-qalam, al-Muddaththir, and al-Ḍuḥā.; cites 68:1, 74:1, 93:1 DURR (أبو موسى الأشعري): Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī says Sūrat Iqraʾ was the first sūra sent down to Muhammad. DURR (بعض علمائهم): Scholars heard by Muḥammad b. ʿAbbād said the opening through 96:5 was revealed at Ḥirāʾ; the sūra’s ending came later as God willed. DURR (عائشة): ʿĀʾisha says this was the first Qurʾānic revelation; al-Ḥākim and al-Bayhaqī are both said to have authenticated this report. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): He says the first Qur'an revelation in Mecca was ‘Read in the name of your Lord who created.’ DURR-FULL (أبو موسى الأشعري): He says ‘Read in the name of your Lord’ was the first sura revealed to Muhammad; al-Hakim authenticated the report. DURR-FULL (ابن شهاب): Scholars he cites say the opening through ‘what he did not know’ was revealed at Hira and the rest came later. DURR-FULL (عائشة): Aisha says the first revelation was ‘Read in the name of your Lord who created.’ DURR-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Aisha's account describes true dreams and retreat at Hira before Gabriel commands him to read, presses him three times, and recites the first five verses; Muhammad returns trembling to Khadija. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): He says ‘Read in the name of your Lord who created’ was the first sura revealed to Muhammad. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): He says the first revelation was ‘Read in the name of your Lord,’ followed by Nun and the Pen.; cites 68:1 DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): He identifies the first thing revealed as five verses, from ‘Read in the name of your Lord’ through ‘what he did not know.’ DURR-FULL (عبيد بن عمير): He says ‘Read in the name of your Lord’ came first, followed by Nun.; cites 68:1 DURR-FULL (عائشة): Aisha reports that after ‘Read in the name of your Lord,’ revelations included Nun and the Pen, al-Muddaththir, and al-Duha.; cites 68:1, 74:1, 93:1 DURR-FULL (عبد الله بن شداد): His account says Gabriel pressed Muhammad, recited the opening through ‘what he did not know,’ and later revelation paused; the descent of al-Duha answered Khadija's worry that God had abandoned him.; cites 93:1 ITQAN: Of the reports about the first revelation, the soundest view is that it began with اقرأ باسم ربك; Aisha’s report in the two Sahih collections recounts the true dreams, retreat at Hira, and Gabriel’s three commands to read through ما لم يعلم.; cites 96:1, 96:5 ITQAN (عائشة): Al-Hakim and al-Bayhaqi authenticate Aisha’s report that اقرأ باسم ربك was the first surah revealed. ITQAN (أبو رجاء العطاردي): Al-Tabarani transmits with a chain meeting the soundness standard Abu Raja’s report that Abu Musa called this the first surah sent to Muhammad. ITQAN (عبيد بن عمير): Ubayd ibn Umayr reports that Gabriel instructed the Prophet to read and then recited اقرأ باسم ربك, which the Prophet called the first revelation. ITQAN (مجاهد): Mujahid says the first revelation included both اقرأ باسم ربك and ن والقلم, an alternative to the preferred report that begins with اقرأ.; cites 68:1 ITQAN (عبيد بن عمير): Ubayd b. ʿUmayr says Gabriel brought the Prophet a silk sheet and told him to recite; he identifies the opening of al-ʿAlaq as the first sura sent down. ITQAN: One reconciliation distinguishes the first complete sura, al-Muddaththir, from the first verses revealed, the beginning of al-ʿAlaq. ITQAN: A second authentic report places al-Muddaththir after the pause in revelation: the Prophet saw the same angel previously encountered at Ḥirāʾ, showing the Ḥirāʾ revelation came earlier. ITQAN: The final reconciliation says al-ʿAlaq was first for prophethood, while al-Muddaththir was first for the mission and command to warn. ITQAN (ابن حجر): Ibn Hajar distinguishes the first revelation prompted by an earlier event—the Prophet's fright and covering himself—from “Read,” which was revealed initially without a prior cause.; cites 74:1 ITQAN (الكرماني): Al-Kirmani argues that Jabir inferred his view rather than narrating it, so Aisha's transmitted report should take precedence. BURSEVI: Iqra is a command, repeated to reinforce the obligation and introduce a new sentence about the most generous Lord; a permissible pause follows خلق because the Prophet had objected that he could not read. ALUSI (عائشة): Another view, supported by ʿĀʾisha's account of the beginning of revelation, says only the opening passage through mā lam yaʿlam came first at Ḥirāʾ, with the rest revealed later.; cites 96:1, 96:2, 96:3, 96:4, 96:5 ALUSI (عائشة): In the beginning-of-revelation report, the angel squeezed the Prophet three times, then recited the opening verses through the command to teach what was not known; the report is transmitted by Aḥmad and both Ṣaḥīḥ compilers.; cites 96:1, 96:2, 96:3, 96:4, 96:5 ALUSI: The wording about the interruption and renewed revelation shows why Jābir's report does not establish al-Muddaththir as first overall: it was first after the pause. ALUSI: Al-Ālūsī's chosen view is that the opening of this sūra was the first Qurʾānic revelation overall, supported by ʿĀʾisha's especially sound report.; cites 96:1, 96:2, 96:3, 96:4, 96:5 ALUSI (النووي): Al-Nawawī says mā in the Prophet's response mā anā bi-qāriʾ is a negation, which fits a first command; otherwise refusing to read after prior instruction would be a grave sin. ALUSI (صاحب الكشف): Al-Kashf reconciles Jābir's report by taking his claim to mean the first complete sūra, while al-Muddaththir was first after the interval. ALUSI (صاحب شرح صحيح مسلم): The commentary on Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim says Iqraʾ was first absolutely and al-Muddaththir first after the pause; it calls the Fātiḥa-first view clearly invalid. ALUSI: Commentators disagree about which revelation came first; many say this sūra was first. ALUSI (أبو رجاء العطاردي): Al-Ṭabarānī reports with a chain meeting the soundness criterion from Abū Rajāʾ al-ʿUṭāridī that Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī said Iqraʾ was the first sūra revealed to Muhammad. ALUSI (عائشة): Al-Ḥākim and al-Bayhaqī authenticated a report from ʿĀʾisha giving the same account that Iqraʾ was first. ALUSI (مجاهد): Mujāhid says the first revelations were Iqraʾ bi-smi rabbik followed by Nūn wa-l-qalam. ALUSI-FULL (أبو رجاء العطاردي): Many say Iqra was the first revelation; Abu Musa's student Abu Raja reports that Abu Musa called it the first surah sent to Muhammad. ALUSI-FULL (عائشة): Al-Hakim and al-Bayhaqi authenticate a report from Aisha likewise identifying Iqra as first; Mujahid says it was followed by Nun and the pen. ALUSI-FULL: Another view holds that only the opening of Iqra through what he did not know was revealed first and the remainder came later, as suggested by Aisha's account of the first revelation. ALUSI-FULL (عائشة): Aisha’s account says revelation paused soon after the opening verses; a report from Jabir then recounts how revelation resumed with al-Muddaththir. ALUSI-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): Jabir's account of Muddaththir explicitly concerns the period after revelation paused and then resumed, undermining its use as the first revelation overall. ALUSI-FULL: Al-Alusi follows the view that the opening of Iqra was the first revelation outright, supported by Aisha's highly authentic report; ما أنا بقارئ is a statement of inability, not refusal, which would otherwise be grave disobedience. ALUSI-FULL (الكشف): Al-Kashf takes Jabir's claim to concern the first complete surah, while Sahih Muslim's commentary distinguishes Iqra as first overall from Muddaththir as first after the hiatus; the claim that al-Fatiha came first is plainly false. QUTB-ZILAL: Qutb says the sura's opening was the first revelation by consensus; accounts assigning that primacy elsewhere are unreliable. QUTB-ZILAL: Only the opening passage dates to the first revelation; the sura's later portions refer to the Prophet's subsequent preaching, public worship, and pagan opposition, yet form a coherent ordered whole. QUTB-ZILAL: As the Qur'an's first sura, it begins with God and directs the Prophet at the first heavenly contact and first step of his mission to read in God's name, beginning with the Creator. QUTB-ZILAL (عائشة): In the report Qutb says is recorded in both Sahih collections, Aisha recounts true dreams preceding revelation, solitary worship at Hira, Gabriel's three commands to read, and the first five verses. QUTB-ZILAL (عبد الله بن الزبير): Al-Tabari's report from Ibn al-Zubayr recounts the Prophet's alternate account: an angel brought written silk during sleep, recited the opening passage, and left it inscribed in his heart; later Gabriel appeared in the sky and identified himself. IBNASHUR (عائشة): Aisha's report is Ibn Ashur's basis for identifying these five verses as the first revelation to Muhammad in Hira. IBNASHUR (عائشة، أبو موسى الأشعري): Sound reports from Aisha establish that these first five verses were first, delivered to Muhammad in Hira on Ramadan 17, year 40 after the Elephant; Abu Musa and most exegetes agree. IBNASHUR: These are the final verses of the five that were first revealed to Muhammad in the cave of Hira. IBNASHUR-FULL: This is the first Qur'an revealed to Muhammad, as established in the forthcoming report from Aisha. IBNASHUR-FULL: These are the final five verses of the passage first revealed at Hira; the following discussion on verses 6–10 begins a new subject. TABATABAI: The wording supports this being the first revelation and implies an omitted object, ‘the Qur’an’ or its equivalent. BINTSHATI: She presents the surah’s opening as the first revelation, noting that Ibn Ishaq and al-Tabari report no disagreement about that account. BINTSHATI (عائشة أم المؤمنين): Aisha recounts Muhammad’s true dreams, retreat to Hira, Gabriel’s command and three embraces, and the recitation of the opening five verses, which remained fixed in his heart; no grading is stated. BINTSHATI (أبو حيان): Al-Bahr al-Muhit says the surah is Meccan and its opening was the first revelation at Hira, as established in Bukhari and elsewhere; it judges the claims that al-Muddaththir or the Fatiha came first unsound. BINTSHATI: The reports in al-Tabari and the Prophetic biography stop the first-revelation account at verse five, supporting the view that the opening alone was first revealed. ISLAHI-TADABBUR (some people): One reported view makes the whole of Sūrah al-‘Alaq the Qur’an’s first revelation; the majority instead regards its first five verses as first, based on a report in Bukhārī and Muslim. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR: These were the first revelation; the command to read addresses Muhammad and all Muslims, and its repetition stresses the importance of reading and knowledge, through which humans are distinguished among living things.; cites 2:31 IBNASHUR-FULL: The surah is Meccan by agreement and was the first revealed; its first five verses came in Hira during Ramadan on the seventeenth, year forty after the Elephant, according to sound Aisha reports and most exegetes.; cites 96:5 Al-Ālūsī reports that ʿĀʾisha's identification of the opening of al-ʿAlaq as the first revelation occurs in both Ṣaḥīḥs and is followed by most authorities; the competing report from Jābir is also sound and calls for reconciliation.
  2. Al-Fātiḥa was first. — Abū Maysara's report is cited by Ibn ʿAṭiyya and al-Qurṭubī; al-Kashshāf and al-Nasafī attribute this to a majority. Jishumī rejects it as contrary to consensus; Abū Ḥayyān judges the report unsound. ALUSI (صاحب شرح صحيح مسلم): The commentary on Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim says Iqraʾ was first absolutely and al-Muddaththir first after the pause; it calls the Fātiḥa-first view clearly invalid. ALUSI (أبو ميسرة عمرو بن شرحبيل): A mursal report with trustworthy narrators, transmitted by al-Bayhaqī and al-Wāḥidī, supports the view that al-Fātiḥa came first. BINTSHATI (الزمخشري والفخر الرازي): An alternative view in al-Kashshaf and al-Razi’s commentary makes the Fatiha the first revealed surah, followed by al-Alaq. ISLAHI-TADABBUR (Zamakhsharī): Zamakhsharī records the view that al-Fātiḥah was revealed first and then Sūrah al-Qalam; exegetes both accept and dispute it. Against—ALUSI: The Fātiḥa-first report could describe what was revealed after Iqraʾ and al-Muddaththir, and the reports supporting the other order are stronger. Against—ALUSI-FULL (أبو ميسرة عمرو بن شرحبيل): A trustworthy-transmitters but mursal report argues that al-Fatiha came first; the response is that it could refer to what followed Iqra and Muddaththir, and other reports are stronger. Against—ALUSI-FULL (الكشف): Al-Kashf takes Jabir's claim to concern the first complete surah, while Sahih Muslim's commentary distinguishes Iqra as first overall from Muddaththir as first after the hiatus; the claim that al-Fatiha came first is plainly false. Against—QUTB-ZILAL: Qutb says the sura's opening was the first revelation by consensus; accounts assigning that primacy elsewhere are unreliable. Against—BINTSHATI (أبو حيان): Al-Bahr al-Muhit says the surah is Meccan and its opening was the first revelation at Hira, as established in Bukhari and elsewhere; it judges the claims that al-Muddaththir or the Fatiha came first unsound.
  3. Al-Muddaththir was first. — Jābir's report is cited by Ibn ʿAṭiyya and al-Qurṭubī; al-Wāḥidī answers that Jābir knew the later encounter after the pause. ALUSI (جابر بن عبد الله): Relating the pause in revelation, Jābir says the angel appeared seated between heaven and earth; the Prophet returned frightened, and God then revealed al-Muddaththir. ALUSI (صاحب الكشف): Al-Kashf reconciles Jābir's report by taking his claim to mean the first complete sūra, while al-Muddaththir was first after the interval. ALUSI (جابر بن عبد الله): In a report in both Ṣaḥīḥ collections, Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh answers that Yā ayyuhā al-muddaththir was first; he responds to those who cite Iqraʾ by narrating the Prophet's account. ALUSI-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): A report from Jabir calls Ya ayyuhal-Muddaththir the first revelation, but the earlier group offers several responses to it. ALUSI-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): Jabir's account of Muddaththir explicitly concerns the period after revelation paused and then resumed, undermining its use as the first revelation overall. ALUSI-FULL (الكشف): Al-Kashf takes Jabir's claim to concern the first complete surah, while Sahih Muslim's commentary distinguishes Iqra as first overall from Muddaththir as first after the hiatus; the claim that al-Fatiha came first is plainly false. IBNASHUR (جابر): Jabir reports that al-Muddaththir was the first surah revealed. IBNASHUR: Ibn Ashur reconciles Jabir's report by taking al-Muddaththir as the first surah after the interruption in revelation, just as al-Duha followed a second interruption, as al-Itqan explains. IBNASHUR-FULL (جابر): Jabir says al-Muddaththir was first; Ibn Ashur reconciles this as the first revelation after the pause, like al-Duha after the second pause. ISLAHI-TADABBUR (the proponents of this view): A third view says Sūrah al-Muddaththir was first; its proponents probably mean only its opening verses because the rest has a severity resembling al-‘Alaq. Against—ITQAN (الكرماني): Al-Kirmani argues that Jabir inferred his view rather than narrating it, so Aisha's transmitted report should take precedence. Against—QUTB-ZILAL: Qutb says the sura's opening was the first revelation by consensus; accounts assigning that primacy elsewhere are unreliable. Against—BINTSHATI (أبو حيان): Al-Bahr al-Muhit says the surah is Meccan and its opening was the first revelation at Hira, as established in Bukhari and elsewhere; it judges the claims that al-Muddaththir or the Fatiha came first unsound. Al-Ālūsī reports Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh's sound narration: after retreat at Ḥirāʾ and the angel's sudden appearance, Muhammad returned frightened and al-Muddaththir was revealed, which Jābir takes to imply its priority over al-ʿAlaq.
  4. The reports use 'first' for different stages: al-ʿAlaq first verses, al-Muddaththir after a pause or first public warning, and al-Fātiḥa as a complete sūra. — Al-Wāḥidī says al-Muddaththir was first after Iqraʾ and its pause. Al-Burhān's Qāḍī Abū Bakr distinguishes first verses, first public command, and first complete sūra; Ibn Ḥibbān locates the later revelation in Khadīja's house. BURHAN: The distinction resembles reports that prayer is the first duty reckoned and bloodshed the first dispute judged: each is first within a different category. BURHAN (بعضهم): Another distinction says اقرأ باسم ربك was first for prophethood, while يا أيها المدثر was first for messengership, since the latter commands a general public warning.; cites 74:1 DURR (جابر بن عبد الله الأنصاري): Jābir recounts the resumed revelation: Gabriel appeared seated between heaven and earth, and God sent the opening of Sūrat al-Muddaththir; revelation then intensified.; cites 74:1, 74:2, 74:3, 74:4, 74:5 ITQAN: One reconciliation distinguishes the first complete sura, al-Muddaththir, from the first verses revealed, the beginning of al-ʿAlaq. ITQAN: A second authentic report places al-Muddaththir after the pause in revelation: the Prophet saw the same angel previously encountered at Ḥirāʾ, showing the Ḥirāʾ revelation came earlier. ITQAN: The final reconciliation says al-ʿAlaq was first for prophethood, while al-Muddaththir was first for the mission and command to warn. ITQAN (ابن حجر): Ibn Hajar distinguishes the first revelation prompted by an earlier event—the Prophet's fright and covering himself—from “Read,” which was revealed initially without a prior cause.; cites 74:1 ITQAN (الكرماني): Al-Kirmani argues that Jabir inferred his view rather than narrating it, so Aisha's transmitted report should take precedence. ALUSI: The wording about the interruption and renewed revelation shows why Jābir's report does not establish al-Muddaththir as first overall: it was first after the pause. ALUSI (صاحب الكشف): Al-Kashf reconciles Jābir's report by taking his claim to mean the first complete sūra, while al-Muddaththir was first after the interval. ALUSI (صاحب شرح صحيح مسلم): The commentary on Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim says Iqraʾ was first absolutely and al-Muddaththir first after the pause; it calls the Fātiḥa-first view clearly invalid. ALUSI (السيوطي): Al-Suyūṭī says the basmala-first claim is not an independent view because the basmala necessarily descended with the sūra, making it the first verse overall; al-Ālūsī calls this reasoning plainly objectionable. ALUSI-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): Jabir's account of Muddaththir explicitly concerns the period after revelation paused and then resumed, undermining its use as the first revelation overall. ALUSI-FULL: Al-Alusi follows the view that the opening of Iqra was the first revelation outright, supported by Aisha's highly authentic report; ما أنا بقارئ is a statement of inability, not refusal, which would otherwise be grave disobedience. ALUSI-FULL (الكشف): Al-Kashf takes Jabir's claim to concern the first complete surah, while Sahih Muslim's commentary distinguishes Iqra as first overall from Muddaththir as first after the hiatus; the claim that al-Fatiha came first is plainly false. IBNASHUR: Ibn Ashur reconciles Jabir's report by taking al-Muddaththir as the first surah after the interruption in revelation, just as al-Duha followed a second interruption, as al-Itqan explains. IBNASHUR-FULL (جابر): Jabir says al-Muddaththir was first; Ibn Ashur reconciles this as the first revelation after the pause, like al-Duha after the second pause. Al-Ālūsī reports a reconciliation in which the complete al-Muddaththir preceded completion of al-ʿAlaq, although al-ʿAlaq's opening came first. He reports that the first and fifth reconciliations—this distinction and treating Jābir's claim as inference rather than transmitted priority—were judged best, while signaling reservations about that judgment.
  5. The basmala preceded al-ʿAlaq. — Al-Wāḥidī reports ʿIkrima and al-Ḥasan saying the basmala preceded Sūrat Iqraʾ. ITQAN (عكرمة والحسن): Ibn al-Naqib mentions a fourth view that the basmala was first revealed; a report from Ikrima and al-Hasan says it was first, while the first sura was “Read.”; cites 96:1 ALUSI (عكرمة والحسن): A report from ʿIkrima and al-Ḥasan says the first Qurʾānic revelation was bismi Allāh al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm, followed by the opening of Iqraʾ.; cites 1:1, 96:1 ALUSI (ابن عباس): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk's report from Ibn ʿAbbās says Gabriel first instructed the Prophet to seek refuge, then to say the basmala. ALUSI (السيوطي): Al-Suyūṭī says the basmala-first claim is not an independent view because the basmala necessarily descended with the sūra, making it the first verse overall; al-Ālūsī calls this reasoning plainly objectionable. ALUSI-FULL (عكرمة، الحسن): Ikrima and Hasan say the basmala was the first Quranic revelation; al-Dahhak's report from Ibn Abbas says Gabriel first instructed Muhammad to seek refuge and say it. Al-Suyuti counts this as the first revelation, but al-Alusi objects that this does not follow.
  6. The command at 6:151 was first. — Al-Qurṭubī transmits ʿAlī's report that 6:151 was the first revelation but prefers the opening sūra.
96:4/q02 · interpretation

Allah burada ne öğretir?

Ayrılık noktası: Kalemin sıradan yazıyı mı, göksel vahyi mi, yoksa daha geniş bir bilgi türünü mü ifade ettiği; genişletilmiş okuyuşta açıkça “yazı” denir.
  1. Allah insanlara kalem aracılığıyla yazıyı ve onun kaydettiği bilgiyi öğretir. — Mukātil, yazıyı Kur'an'ı öğretmeyle de birleştirir. Kelbî, “yazı veya yazılı kitap” olmasına izin verir; Ebû İshak, insanların yazmasını kasteder. Taberî, Zaccâc, Sa'lebî, Mâverdî, Vâhidî, Bağavî, Nesefî ve başkaları doğrudan yazı veya el yazısı açıklamasını verir. Cüşemî, koruma ve öğretim işlevi nedeniyle bunu tercih eder. İbnü'z-Zübeyr'in genişletilmiş lafzı “yazı”yı ekler; Kurtubî ve Bikāî bunu ilahî cömertlikle ilişkilendirir. Tabâtabâî, okuma veya yazma öğretme ihtimalini kabul eder.
    45 not: Muqātil b. Sulaymān; al-Ṭabarī; al-Zajjāj; al-Thaʿlabī; al-Māwardī; al-Māwardī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Ḥākim al-Jishumī+; al-Baghawī; al-Baghawī; al-Ṭabrisī; Ibn al-Jawzī; Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; al-Qurṭubī; al-Qurṭubī; al-Bayḍāwī; al-Bayḍāwī; al-Nasafī; al-Nasafī; al-Zarkashī; al-Biqāʿī; al-Suyūṭī; İsmāʿīl Ḥaqqī Bursevī; al-Ālūsī; al-Ālūsī; Elmalılı Muhammed Hamdi Yazır; Muḥammad Ḥusayn al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī; ʿĀʾisha ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (Bint al-Shāṭiʾ)+; Nicolai Sinai (Frühmekkanische Suren), with Nora K. Schmid, using preparatory work by Angelika Neuwirth; later parts Dirk Hartwig, Angelika Neuwirth et al. (author line per sura in segments); Hayreddin Karaman, Mustafa Çağrıcı, İbrahim Kâfi Dönmez, Sadrettin Gümüş
  2. Kalem, başka türlü bilinmeyecek veya gizli kalacak hususları aktaran yazıyı temsil eder. — Râzî, bunu kitap yazmayı öğretmeye ilişkin diğer doğrudan açıklamasından ayırarak açıkça mecaz-ı mürsel sayar; ikisi de yazının değerini gösterir. Corpus Coranicum da “kalemle öğretmeyi”, yazı öğretmekten ayrı bir anlam olarak kabul eder.
    3 not: Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; Nicolai Sinai (Frühmekkanische Suren), with Nora K. Schmid, using preparatory work by Angelika Neuwirth; later parts Dirk Hartwig, Angelika Neuwirth et al. (author line per sura in segments)
  3. Öğretme, vahyedilmiş dinî bilgiyi veya Levh-i Mahfuz'da kaydedilmiş Allah kelâmını kapsar. — Râzî, yaratılışın aklî delilini nakledilmiş hükümler ve peygamberlikle karşılaştırır. Ebû Ali, meleklerin Allah'ın kelâmını Levh-i Mahfuz'dan okuyup ilettiğini söyler; Neuwirth, kalemi göksel sayar ve 85:22, 68:1 ve 80:13-16'ya atıfta bulunur. Bintü'ş-Şâti, Nîsâbûrî'nin benzer simgesel karşıtlığını aktarır ama onu bir kenara bırakır.
    6 not: Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; al-Ḥākim al-Jishumī; ʿĀʾisha ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (Bint al-Shāṭiʾ); Nicolai Sinai (Frühmekkanische Suren), with Nora K. Schmid, using preparatory work by Angelika Neuwirth; later parts Dirk Hartwig, Angelika Neuwirth et al. (author line per sura in segments) · karşı: ʿĀʾisha ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (Bint al-Shāṭiʾ)
  4. Öğretme, gerekli bilgi ve akıl dâhil bütün insan öğrenimini kapsar. — Kuşeyrî, zorunlu ve kazanılmış bilgiyi kapsar. Cüşemî; bütün dinî ve şer'î bilgiyi, tüm insan öğrenimini, aklı olgunlaştıran zorunlu bilimleri ve akıl yürütmeyi alternatifler olarak aktarır.
    2 not: al-Qushayrī; al-Ḥākim al-Jishumī
  5. Öğretme, Âdem'e öğretilen ve soyundan gelenlere miras kalan isimleri kapsar. — Cüşemî bu açıklamayı aktarır; İbn Fâris de yazı harflerini Allah'ın Âdem'e öğrettiği isimlerle ilişkilendirir.
    2 not: al-Ḥākim al-Jishumī; al-Suyūṭī
İngilizce aslı

What does God teach here?

Turns on: Whether the pen refers to ordinary writing, heavenly scripture, or a wider kind of knowledge; the expanded reading explicitly supplies 'writing'.

  1. God teaches human beings writing and the knowledge it records through a pen. — Muqātil also joins writing to teaching the Qur'an. Al-Kalbī allows 'writing or the written book'; Abū Isḥāq specifies humans writing. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Zajjāj, al-Thaʿlabī, al-Māwardī, al-Wāḥidī, al-Baghawī, al-Nasafī, and others give the straightforward script or handwriting gloss. Al-Jishumī prefers it for preservation and instruction. Ibn al-Zubayr's expanded wording supplies 'writing'; al-Qurṭubī and al-Biqāʿī link it to divine generosity. Al-Ṭabāṭabāʾi allows teaching reading or writing.
  2. The pen stands for writing that conveys otherwise absent or hidden matters. — Al-Rāzī explicitly calls this a metonym, contrasted with his other direct gloss of teaching book writing; both show writing's excellence. Corpus Coranicum also allows 'teaching with a pen' as distinct from teaching writing.
  3. The teaching is revealed religious knowledge or God's speech recorded in the heavenly Tablet. — Al-Rāzī contrasts the rational sign of creation with transmitted rulings and prophethood. Abū ʿAlī says angels read God's speech from the Preserved Tablet and convey it; Neuwirth takes the pen as heavenly and invokes 85:22, 68:1, and 80:13–16. Bint al-Shāṭiʾ reports al-Nīsābūrī's similar symbolic contrast but sets it aside.
  4. The teaching encompasses all human learning, including necessary knowledge and reason. — Al-Qushayrī includes necessary and acquired knowledge. Al-Jishumī reports alternatives of all religious and legal knowledge, all human learning, and necessary sciences and reasoning that perfect intellect.
  5. The teaching is the names taught to Adam and inherited by his descendants. — Al-Jishumī reports this explanation; Ibn Fāris also links letters of script to the names God taught Adam.
96:5/q01 · interpretation

Allah insana daha önce bilmediği neyi öğretti?

Ayrılık noktası: Kalem cümlesinin öğretmeyi yazıyla sınırlayıp sınırlamadığı, bunun daha geniş bilgileri kapsayıp kapsamadığı ve alıcının adı verilmiş bir kişi olup olmadığı.
  1. Kalemle yazı ve yazı biçimi. — İbn Zeyd ve Katâde bunu açıkça yazı olarak açıklar; Kelbî kitapta yazı olduğunu belirtir. İbnü'z-Zübeyr'in açıklayıcı okuyuşu yazı biçimini adlandırır; İbn Abbas'a da bu açıklamanın nispet edildiği bildirilir.
    15 not: al-Ṭabarī; al-Ṭabarī; Ibn Abī Ḥātim al-Rāzī; al-Māwardī; al-Māwardī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Zamakhsharī; al-Ālūsī; al-Suyūṭī; al-Suyūṭī; ʿĀʾisha ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (Bint al-Shāṭiʾ)
  2. Yazı, hidayet, din, zanaatlar ve kavrayış dâhil daha önce bilinmeyen bütün bilgiler. — Taberî, yazının yanı sıra bilinmeyen başka şeyleri de ekler. Sa'lebî ve Bağavî hidayet ile ifadeyi anar; Tabrisî din, hukuk ve hükümleri ekler. Kuşeyrî zorunlu ve kazanılmış bilgiyi; İbnü'l-Cevzî yazı ve zanaatları; Bursevî genel, özel, açık ve gizli bilgiyi kapsar. Bikāî, Kitap'ı, Sünnet'i, muameleleri, akıl yürütmeyi ve doğrudan kavrayışı ekler; sıradan öğrenme tek başına kişilerin farklı yeteneklerini ve öğrenme sürelerini açıklamaz. Âlûsî, yazıyla ve yazısız öğretmeyi kapsar; Kutub bütün keşifleri ekler.
    25 not: Muqātil b. Sulaymān; al-Ṭabarī; al-Ṭabarī; al-Thaʿlabī; al-Baghawī; al-Baghawī; al-Qushayrī; al-Ṭabrisī; Ibn al-Jawzī; Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī; İsmāʿīl Ḥaqqī Bursevī; al-Ālūsī; al-Ālūsī; al-Biqāʿī; al-Biqāʿī; Ibn ʿĀshūr; Ibn ʿĀshūr; ʿĀʾisha ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (Bint al-Shāṭiʾ); Sayyid Quṭb; Elmalılı Muhammed Hamdi Yazır
  3. İnsanların öğrendiği her zanaat. — İbn Şecere bu açıklamayı verir.
    2 not: al-Māwardī; al-Māwardī
  4. İnsanın ilk yaratılışında, Yaratıcısının ve onu dönüştürme kudretinin kanıtı. — Mâverdî, yaratılışın kendisini öğretim olarak önerir.
    2 not: al-Māwardī; al-Māwardī
  5. Âdem'e öğretilen her şeyin adları. — Müfessirler 2:31'i aktarır. Kurtubî, Âdem'in adları meleklere göstererek mertebesini ve peygamberliğini kanıtladığını söyler; soyundan gelenler onun bilgisini miras almıştır. Vâhidî, bu yorumun beşinci ayeti kalem cümlesinden ayırdığına dikkat çeker.
    9 not: al-Thaʿlabī; al-Baghawī; al-Baghawī; al-Wāḥidī; al-Qurṭubī; al-Qurṭubī; Seyyed Hossein Nasr (ed.-in-chief), Caner K. Dagli, Maria Massi Dakake, Joseph E. B. Lumbard, Mohammed Rustom
  6. Muhammed'e öğretilen Kur'an ve önceden bilinmeyen şeriat, hükümler, kıssalar veya Kitap ve Hikmet. — Mukātil Kur'an'ı belirtir; başkaları 4:113'ü aktarır. Pasaj erken döneme ait olduğundan Kurtubî bu öğretmeyi geleceğe yerleştirir; Vâhidî, Muhammed'in o sırada az şey bilmesi nedeniyle bu yorumu zayıf bulur.
    7 not: Muqātil b. Sulaymān; al-Thaʿlabī; al-Baghawī; al-Baghawī; al-Qurṭubī; al-Qurṭubī; Seyyed Hossein Nasr (ed.-in-chief), Caner K. Dagli, Maria Massi Dakake, Joseph E. B. Lumbard, Mohammed Rustom · karşı: al-Wāḥidī
  7. Özellikle ilk muhatap olan ümmî Araplara verilen eksiksiz hidayet. — Islâhî bunu 62:2 ile ilişkilendirir ve muhatapların bu nimete değer vermesi gerektiğini söyler.
    2 not: Amin Ahsan Islahi (d. 1997)
  8. Peygamber vahyiyle verilen manevi hakikatler ve ahlaki ölçütler. — Esed, insan deneyimi ve aklının bunları tek başına kesin biçimde ortaya koyamayacağını söyler.
    1 not: Muhammad Asad
İngilizce aslı

What did God teach the human that was previously unknown?

Turns on: Whether the pen clause limits the teaching to writing or this covers broader knowledge, and whether the recipient is a named person.

  1. Writing and script with the pen. — Ibn Zayd and Qatāda explicitly gloss it as writing; al-Kalbī specifies writing in a book. Ibn al-Zubayr's explanatory reading names script; Ibn ʿAbbās is also reported to give this gloss.
  2. All formerly unknown knowledge, including writing, guidance, religion, crafts and insight. — Ṭabarī adds other unknown things to writing. Al-Thaʿlabī and Baghawī name guidance and expression; Ṭabrisī adds religion, law and rulings. Qushayrī includes necessary and acquired knowledge; Ibn al-Jawzī writing and crafts; Bursawī universal, particular, manifest and hidden knowledge. Biqāʿī adds Scripture, Sunna, dealings, reasoning and immediate insight; ordinary learning alone would not explain unequal ability and learning time. Ālūsī includes teaching with and without writing; Qutb all discoveries.
  3. Every craft people learn. — Ibn Shajara gives this explanation.
  4. Evidence from humanity's initial creation of its Creator and His power to move it. — Al-Māwardī proposes creation itself as the instruction.
  5. The names of everything taught to Adam. — The commentators cite 2:31. Qurṭubī says Adam displayed the names to angels, demonstrating his rank and prophethood; descendants inherited his knowledge. Wāḥidī notes that this detaches verse 5 from the pen clause.
  6. The Qur'an and previously unknown law, rulings, stories or Book and Wisdom taught to Muhammad. — Muqātil names the Qur'an; others cite 4:113. Qurṭubī places this teaching in the future because the passage was early; Wāḥidī calls the reading weak since Muhammad then knew little.
  7. Complete guidance especially for the unlettered Arab first audience. — Islāḥī links 62:2 and says the audience must honor this favor.
  8. Spiritual truths and moral standards given through prophetic revelation. — Asad says human experience and reason alone cannot establish them unequivocally.
